About this text

Toh 7 in the Kangyur is the Vinayottaragrantha — known in Sanskrit as the Vinayottaragrantha, in Tibetan as Dulwa Zhung Lama (འདུལ་བ་གཞུང་བླ་མ་), and rendered into English as The Supreme Text of the Vinaya. It is a Vinaya text, belonging to the broad class of monastic disciplinary literature that governs the rules and conduct of the Buddhist community of ordained practitioners. The text takes the form of a dialogue between the Buddha and the venerable Upāli, one of the Buddha's chief disciples and the foremost authority on monastic discipline, addressing detailed questions about the proper interpretation and application of the rules of training. As Toh 7 in the Kangyur, it belongs to the Vinaya section of the Tibetan Buddhist canon, which preserves the foundational codes of monastic law transmitted within the Mūlasarvāstivāda tradition.

Translation

In the Sanskrit language: Vinayottaragrantha. In the Tibetan language: The Supreme Text of the Vinaya. The First Fascicle.

Homage to the Omniscient One.

Summary verse:

Three animals, three passages, and
A woman departed, and severance,
Holding and repeated — these,
Together with a tooth, not touching, and a sow.

The Blessed One, the buddha, was dwelling at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove, the garden of Anāthapiṇḍada.

Then the venerable Upāli approached the Blessed One, went to where he was, and bowing his head at the feet of the Blessed One, sat down to one side. Having sat to one side, the venerable Upāli addressed the Blessed One, asking: "Reverend Blessed One, you have declared that any monk who, while sharing equally in the training together with other monks, neither surrenders the training nor declares it to be diminished, yet engages in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse — behavior of unchastity — even if it be with a being reborn in the animal realm, and even alongside such a being, that monk too incurs defeat and is not to remain. With regard to what you have said, Reverend One: suppose a monk, by means of secret mantras and medicines, transforms a human woman into a being reborn in the animal realm and then engages with her in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat." "Reverend One, suppose a monk, by means of secret mantras and medicines, transforms himself into a being reborn in the animal realm and then engages with a human woman in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity — what does that become?" "If he perceives himself as a monk and understands that to engage in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse would be improper, yet does so nonetheless, it becomes a defeat. If he does not perceive himself as a monk, it becomes a grave offense." "Reverend One, suppose two monks, by means of secret mantras and medicines, transform a human woman and themselves into beings reborn in the animal realm, and then together engage mutually in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity — what does that become?" "If one perceives himself as a monk and understands that to engage in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse would be improper, yet does so nonetheless, it becomes a defeat. If one does not perceive himself as a monk, it becomes a grave offense."

The venerable Upāli then asked the Blessed One: "Reverend One, with regard to your declaration that as soon as one has entered it, it becomes a defeat — how much entry constitutes entry into the oral passage?" "Upāli, as much as is required for a jewel to pass from a tooth." "How much entry constitutes entry into the excretory passage?" "As much as is required for a jewel to pass from the hollow of a bone." "How much entry constitutes entry into the urinary passage?" "As much as is required for a jewel to pass through the layers of skin."

"Reverend One, suppose a woman has been cut in two at the middle, and all three of her wound-openings are damaged — if a monk joins wound-opening to wound-opening and engages in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity, what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense." "Reverend One, suppose a woman has been severed at the waist, and one of the three wound-openings — whichever it may be — is undamaged: if a monk engages with her in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity, what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat." "Reverend One, suppose a monk engages together with a woman who has been decapitated in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity — what does that become?" "If it is through the excretory passage or the urinary passage, it becomes a defeat. If it is through the oral passage, the hollow, or the hollow of the shoulder, separately, what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Reverend One, the Blessed One declared that a monk who engages with a non-human female in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity, incurs a defeat — what sort of non-human female is meant?" "Any female that can be grasped with all her limbs. The same is to be said likewise for all those belonging to the animal realm of rebirth."

"Reverend One, suppose a monk inserts into the interior of a mouth outside the teeth — engaging in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Reverend One, suppose a monk does so without touching either the excretory passage, the urinary passage, or the oral passage at all — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Reverend One, suppose a monk engages in the practice arisen from sexual intercourse, the behavior of unchastity, with the burned and hair-free corpse of a sow — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense. The same is likewise to be said for all those belonging to the animal realm of rebirth."

The summary verse:

Another's limb born; the two privies and the two organs;
the two impairments; with obstruction —
sets of six and sets of four.

"Reverend One, suppose a monk inserts into another's formed limb — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Reverend One, is there a case where a monk who has entered a privy alone can incur a defeat on account of the behavior of unchastity?" "There can be something approximating it — for example, like a creditor and a co-guarantor."

"Reverend One, suppose a monk has entered a room, and on the far side beyond it there is a woman, and she engages in misconduct from within — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Reverend One, suppose a monk is in the midst of engaging in the behavior of unchastity, his status changes, and having become a laywoman he then performs the behavior of unchastity — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Reverend One, suppose a nun is in the midst of engaging in the behavior of unchastity, her status changes, and having become a monk she then performs the behavior of unchastity — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Reverend One, what did the Blessed One mean by declaring 'a living human woman who is impaired'? How impaired must a living human woman be for her to be called impaired?" "However much the region nearby is slightly consumed, or consumed, or swollen, or decayed, or putrefied, or eaten by insects — to that extent she is called a living human woman who is impaired."

"How unimpaired must she be for her to be called a living human woman who is not impaired?" "However much the region nearby is even and smooth on both sides — to that same degree she is called unimpaired. The same is likewise to be said for a deceased woman, a non-human female, one belonging to the animal realm of rebirth, a man, a hermaphrodite (paṇḍaka), and a female hermaphrodite."

"Reverend One, the Blessed One declared that if insertion occurs with an obstruction it becomes a defeat — what does that mean? Reverend One, suppose a monk wraps in thick cloth or in a rag and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"But if he wraps in fine linen, silk, or thin cloth and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Reverend One, suppose a monk perceives the passage as a passage and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Suppose he perceives the passage as not a passage and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Suppose he has doubt about the passage and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Suppose he perceives what is not a passage as not a passage and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Suppose he perceives what is not a passage as a passage and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Suppose he has doubt about what is not a passage and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Suppose a monk perceives an interrupted passage as interrupted and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense." "Suppose he perceives an interrupted passage as uninterrupted and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense." "Suppose he perceives an uninterrupted passage as interrupted and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat." "Suppose he perceives an uninterrupted passage as uninterrupted and inserts — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Venerable one, is there any case in which a monk who engages in sexual misconduct with a human woman does not incur a defeat?" "Yes. If a transgression had previously arisen, or if he dwells in a state of concealment, or if from the outset he is incompatible, or if she is a hermaphrodite, or if he engages in sexual misconduct after a nun has been expelled — in these cases it becomes a wrongdoing." "Just as a monk incurs a defeat on account of engaging in sexual misconduct, likewise it is a defeat for one who has given up the training and for a nun as well. For a probationer nun, a novice monk, and a novice nun, it is a wrongdoing."

Summary verse: The group of eighty, precious stones, items of no fixed value, the sign, full ordination arranged in groups of seven, and dice — these make twelve.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk takes something belonging to others — whether dwelling in a village or in a forest — that constitutes taking what is not given and amounts to theft, and that if that monk is apprehended by a king or a great minister who says to him, 'You are a thief! You are a fool! You are a dunce! You are a robber!' and kills him, imprisons him, or banishes him — a monk who takes in such a manner has incurred a defeat and must not remain in the community. Regarding this: if a monk counts himself as belonging to both groups of the community of eighty, what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing that is a grave offense." "From what does the lawful portion for him arise?" "From the first." "If, having received it, he were to deny it, what does that become?" "It becomes an offense entailing confession through knowingly speaking falsely." "When the transaction is complete, the value is to be reckoned."

"Venerable one, suppose people have arranged a display of precious stones, and then a monk, with intent to steal, hides a certain precious stone of great value by moving it from a small place to another place — what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense." "Suppose, with intent to steal, he hides a certain precious stone of small value by moving it from a large place to another place — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Venerable one, if a monk steals an object of no monetary value, what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat." "What is an object of no monetary value? That which, by reason of being extremely precious, cannot have a price set upon it. And that which, by reason of being extremely small, is not worth setting a price upon — that too is of no monetary value. If he steals such a thing, what does that become?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk steals an object without having assessed its value — what does that become?" "If the object is complete in all respects, it becomes a defeat. If it is not complete, it becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk has made preparations to steal, and in the course of that he is recognized and, having been identified as a monk, he steals — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Venerable one, suppose a nun has made preparations to steal, and in the course of that she is recognized and, having been identified as a nun, she steals — what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Venerable one, suppose one who has not received full ordination makes preparations to steal, and, not having received full ordination, steals — what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing." "Suppose one who has not received full ordination makes preparations to steal, and —"

— and having received full ordination steals: what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing." "Suppose one who has not received full ordination makes preparations to steal, and then receives full ordination and steals: what does that become?" "It becomes a serious offense." "Suppose one who has received full ordination makes preparations to steal, and, still as one who has received full ordination, steals: what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing." "Suppose one who has received full ordination makes preparations to steal, and, still as one who has received full ordination, steals: what does that become?" "It becomes a serious offense." "Suppose one who has received full ordination makes preparations to steal, and, as one who has received full ordination, steals: what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat." "Suppose one who has received full ordination makes preparations to steal, and one who has not received full ordination steals: what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk retrieves dice from the sky: what does that become?" "It is as the object itself stands." "If he retrieves them by supernatural power, there is no offense."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk retrieves dice from a place that is not a customs post: what does that become?" "If it passes beyond the boundary, it becomes a defeat. If it does not pass beyond the boundary, it becomes a serious offense."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk lends out goods belonging to others that have not been assessed for customs, and retrieves them: what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat." "Suppose he places them inside a needle case and retrieves them: what does that become?" "It becomes a defeat."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk says to merchants, 'Come this way, go this way': what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing." "Suppose a monk speaks to merchants by means of a stratagem: what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing."

"Venerable one, suppose goods belonging to merchants that are subject to customs duty have not yet reached the customs post, and a merchant says to a monk, 'Noble one, please call them back from the customs post and give them to me,' and the monk calls them back: what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing."

"Venerable one, suppose goods belonging to merchants that are subject to customs duty have not yet reached the customs post, and a merchant says to a monk, 'Noble one, please call them back from the customs post and give them to me, and I will give you half of whatever goods there are at the customs post' — and the monk calls them back: what does that become?" "It becomes a serious offense."

"Venerable one, suppose goods belonging to merchants that are subject to customs duty have not yet reached the customs post, and a merchant says to a monk, 'Noble one, please call them back from the customs post and give them to me, and I will give you all of whatever goods there are at the customs post' — and the monk calls them back: what does that become?" "It becomes a serious offense."

"Venerable one, suppose goods belonging to merchants that are subject to customs duty have not yet reached the customs post, and a monk shows them a different road: what does that become?" "It becomes a wrongdoing."

"Venerable one, suppose goods belonging to merchants that are subject to customs duty have already reached the customs post, and a merchant says to a monk, 'Noble one, please call them back from the customs post' — and the monk calls them back: what does that become?" "The value of the customs goods is to be assessed."

"Venerable one, suppose goods belonging to merchants that are subject to customs duty have already reached the customs post, and a merchant says to a monk, 'Noble one, please call them back from the customs post, and I will give you half of whatever goods there are at the customs post' — and the monk calls them back: what does that become?" "The value of the customs goods is to be assessed."

"Venerable one, suppose goods belonging to merchants that are subject to customs duty have already reached the customs post, and a merchant says to a monk, 'Noble one, please call them back from the customs post, and I will give you all of whatever goods there are at the customs post' — and the monk calls them back: what does that become?"

The value of the customs goods is to be assessed.

Summary verse:

Four cases concerning the robe-rack and robes,
three concerning theft and the māṣaka coin,
three concerning the accomplice and two concerning intention,
and the islands — for the sake of others, these are not [downfalls].

Venerable one, suppose a monk, with the intention to steal, collapses the upper part of a robe-rack belonging to another, and then crushes the lower part with the perception that it is his own — what does that become? For the first act there is a grave offence. For the second there is no downfall.

Venerable one, suppose a monk, with the perception that it is his own, collapses the upper part of a robe-rack belonging to another, and then, with the intention to steal, crushes the lower part — what does that become? For the second act there is a grave offence. For the first there is no downfall.

Venerable one, suppose a monk, with the intention to steal, is in the act of stealing along with a robe-rack — and while robes are being draped over that very robe-rack, he carries off the robe-rack together with the robes — what does that become? The value of the robe-rack is to be assessed.

Venerable one, suppose robes have been draped over a robe-rack, and four monks, or more than four, steal them — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence. But if, when divided up individually, each one receives less than five māṣakas, it becomes a defeat.

Venerable one, how many kinds of theft are there? There are six related kinds: theft by not giving, theft by force, theft by causing to be returned, theft by concealment, theft by thorough examination, and theft of the Dharma.

Venerable one, of the six kinds of theft taught by the Blessed One, which of these brings about a defeat? All the remaining kinds except theft of the Dharma bring about a defeat.

Venerable one, concerning what the Blessed One taught — that stealing five māṣakas brings about a defeat — is there a case in which a monk steals three māṣakas and yet a defeat results? Yes, there is: if three māṣakas complete a kārṣāpaṇa coin.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk steals five māṣakas and yet no defeat results? Yes: if ten māṣakas, or more than ten, make up a kārṣāpaṇa.

Venerable one, concerning what the Blessed One taught — that stealing five māṣakas brings about a defeat — how many māṣakas of what kind of nānaka coin, when stolen, bring about a defeat? Twenty māṣakas that complete a nānaka coin. What is a māṣaka? It is what is completed by four kākaṇis. What is a kārṣāpaṇa? It is one made by King Peacock.

Venerable one, concerning what the Blessed One taught — that stealing five māṣakas brings about a defeat — is there a case in which a monk steals a thousand kārṣāpaṇas and yet no defeat results? Yes: if he steals with the perception that they are abandoned property.

Venerable one, concerning what the Blessed One taught — that stealing five māṣakas brings about a defeat — is there a case in which a monk steals five māṣakas and yet no defeat results? Yes: if he steals one at a time, or two at a time, or three at a time, or four at a time — so that the number at any one theft is never five.

Venerable one, suppose a monk takes a valuable article with the perception that it is his own, and then conceals it with the intention to steal — what does that become? It becomes a defeat.

But if, venerable one, he takes it with the intention to steal, and then conceals it with the perception that it is his own — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence.

Venerable one, suppose a monk, while wondering and doubting — "Is this his? Is this not his? Is that this one's? Is that not this one's?" — steals it all the same — what does that become? It becomes a defeat.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals without permission from the western continent of Cattle-Enjoyment — what does that become? If, reckoning by their standard of value, he steals five māṣakas, it becomes a defeat.

Venerable one, suppose he steals without permission from the eastern continent of Videha — what does that become? If, reckoning by their standard of value, he steals five māṣakas, it becomes a defeat.

Venerable one, suppose he steals without permission from the northern continent of Unpleasant Sound — what does that become? In that surrounding region there is no taking what is not given. But if the intention to steal were to arise, it becomes a grave offence.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals on behalf of another without having been asked — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence.

Venerable one, suppose a monk, perceiving as human something that is not human, steals it without permission — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. Suppose he perceives as not human something that is not human, and steals it without permission — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence. Suppose he perceives as not human something that is human, and steals it without permission — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence. Suppose he perceives as human something that is human, and steals it without permission — what does that become? It becomes a defeat.

Now a summary verse:

A reliquary, bodily relics, a scripture text,
the Saṅgha in numbers direct or through a go-between,
the two perceptions, and likewise the status of the thing,
and the household of the king — that is the last.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals an ornament from a stūpa or from the precincts of a stūpa — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals the bodily relics of the Teacher — what does that become? It becomes a defeat. But if he takes them thinking, "This is my own Teacher; I too should show reverence to these relics" — and steals them for the purpose of veneration — it becomes a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals a scripture — what does that become? The value of the scripture is to be assessed.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals without permission from the Saṅgha of the four directions — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals without permission from the property belonging jointly to many persons — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence. But if five māṣakas come from each individual share, it becomes a defeat.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals without permission something that has been inspected and set aside — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, suppose a monk steals without permission something that has passed through many hands — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence. But if it is divided up, the value of however much he has appropriated as his own is to be assessed accordingly.

Venerable one, suppose a monk perceives something as belonging to another and steals it without prompting — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence.

Venerable one, suppose a monk perceives as accepted what has indeed been accepted, and steals it without permission — what does that become? It becomes a defeat. Suppose he perceives as not accepted what has in fact been accepted, and steals it without permission — what does that become? There is no transgression. Suppose he perceives as accepted what has not been accepted, and steals it without permission — what does that become? It becomes a grave offence. Suppose he perceives as not accepted what has not been accepted, and steals it without permission — what does that become? There is no offence.

Venerable one, concerning the Blessed One's statement, "If a monk moves a valuable article from its place, it becomes a defeat" — what exactly is meant by that? Venerable one, even if a monk moves a valuable article from its place, could there be an instance where it does not become a defeat? Yes — if he keeps the article in the very same location where it had been placed and steals it from there.

Venerable one, suppose a monk, concerning something found that does not belong to him, says aloud, "This is within my own power" — what does that become? By knowingly speaking falsely, he incurs a transgression.

If a thieving intent comes to be present in the act, it constitutes a serious offense — a wrongdoing — and once it is complete, the value must be assessed.

Venerable one, could there be an instance where a monk who has entered a house without permission incurs a defeat on account of the condition of taking without being given? Yes — if he makes the effort to steal before entering, or if he makes the effort after having entered.

Venerable one, could there be an instance where a monk takes a major valuable article without being given it and yet does not incur a defeat? Yes — where a transgression has already occurred beforehand; where one dwells in a mode of stealth; where from the outset there is incompatibility; and in the case of a eunuch or a nun who has been expelled — in these cases it becomes a wrongdoing.

Just as a monk may incur a defeat through the condition of taking without being given, so too is it the same for one who has been given the training and for a nun — it is also a defeat. For a probationer, a novice monk, and a novice nun, it is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:
Three animal births and the existence of action,
Craving as mother — a group of four —
Two wombs and a caretaker,
And for perception — also a group of four.

Then the venerable attendant asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared, "Whatever monk, with deliberate intent, kills with his own hand a human being or one who has assumed human form — or strikes him with a weapon, or procures a weapon for him, or causes him to die, or speaks praise of death to him, saying such words as: 'O man, what use is this wretched, impure, evil life to you? O man, it is better for you to die than to live' — and who through such mental desire and mental conceptual elaboration induces him to die or speaks praise of death to him by many different means, and through the act of such instigation causes his death — that monk too is one who has been defeated and must not remain." What exactly is meant by this?

Venerable one, first: if a monk uses secret mantras and medicines to transform a human being into an animal birth and that person is separated from his life — what does that become? It is a defeat.

Venerable one, if a monk uses secret mantras and medicines to transform himself into an animal birth and is then separated from his own life — what does that become? If he has the perception that it is not permissible to kill a monk and yet does it, it is a defeat. If he does not have the perception of a monk, it is a serious offense.

Venerable one, could there be an instance where a monk kills his father or mother and yet does not incur a defeat, and where not even a transgression of immediate consequence touches him? Yes — if the father or mother has taken birth in an animal rebirth.

Venerable one, could there be an instance where a monk kills his father and yet does not incur a defeat, and where not even a transgression of immediate consequence touches him? Yes — if he kills the existence of action accompanied by defilement. The venerable attendant replied: The existence of action accompanied by defilement is called "father" in the sense of manifesting or producing.

Venerable one, could there be an instance where a monk kills his mother and yet does not incur a defeat, and where not even a transgression of immediate consequence touches him? Yes — if he kills craving. The venerable attendant replied: Craving is called "mother" in the sense of giving rise to birth.

Venerable one, if a monk intends to kill his mother but instead kills another woman — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk intends to kill another woman but does not kill his mother — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk engages in the preparation intending to kill his mother, and another woman is separated from her life — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk engages in the preparation intending to kill another woman, and his mother is separated from her life — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk kills a being born in an animal rebirth within a human woman's womb — what does that become? It is an offense.

Venerable one, if a monk kills a human being within the womb of one born in an animal rebirth — what does that become? It is a defeat.

Venerable one, if one person conceives the child and another nurses it — when one of them is separated from life, it is a defeat, and one is also touched by the fault of an action without interval. By whom was the child engendered?

Venerable one, if a monk kills someone while entertaining doubt and uncertainty, thinking "is this that person or is this not that person?" — what does that become? It is a defeat.

Venerable one, if a monk perceives a human being as not human and kills that being — what does that become? It is a defeat.

If a monk perceives a human being as not human and kills that being — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

If a monk perceives a non-human as human and kills that being — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

If a monk perceives a non-human as non-human and kills that being — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Summary verse:

Two states of mind and two regarding self,
And four likewise concerning the arhat,
Two thieves together with other perceptions —
Seven as a group, and the house.

Venerable one, if a monk, while entertaining doubt and uncertainty, thinking "is this that person or is this not that person?" — separates even that being from life — what does that become? It is a defeat.

Venerable one, if a monk separates himself from life — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk has made preparations to kill his father and mother, and then kills himself — what does that become? If the father and mother died first, it is a defeat, and one is also touched by the fault of an action without interval. But if he himself died first, it is a serious offense, and one is not touched by the fault of an action without interval.

Venerable one, if a monk intends to kill an arhat but kills an ordinary person — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk intends to kill an ordinary person but kills an arhat — what does that become? It is a serious offense, yet one is not touched by the fault of an action without interval.

Venerable one, if a monk has made preparations to kill an arhat, and a trainee is separated from life — what does that become? It is a defeat, and one is also touched by the fault of both an action without interval and a proximate fault.

Venerable one, if a monk has made preparations to kill an ordinary person, and an arhat is separated from life — what does that become? It is a defeat, and one is also touched by the fault of an action without interval.

Venerable one, suppose a thief being led away to execution escapes, and the townspeople or the king's men pursue him from behind; and a monk who is coming from ahead is met by those men, who ask him: "Did you see a thief who fled?" — and the monk, his mind close to the intention to kill the thief, points that thief out to them, and that thief is then separated from life — what does that become? It is a defeat.

Venerable one, suppose a thief being led away to execution escapes, and the townspeople or the king's men pursue him from behind; and a monk who is coming from ahead is met by those men, who ask him: "Did you see a thief who fled?" — and the monk, his mind not being close to the intention to kill the thief, points that thief out to them, and that thief is then separated from life — what does that become? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable one, if among two people a monk has made preparations to kill one and kills the other — what does that become? It is a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk makes preparations to kill a fully ordained person and kills one who is not fully ordained — what does that become? It is a wrong deed.

If a monk makes preparations to kill one who is not fully ordained and —

— kills a fully ordained person, what does that become? It is a wrong deed.

If a monk makes preparations to kill one who is not fully ordained and kills someone who has become fully ordained, what does that become? It is a serious offense.

If a monk makes preparations to kill a fully ordained person and kills a fully ordained person, what does that become? It is a wrong deed.

If a monk makes preparations to kill a fully ordained person and kills someone who has become fully ordained, what does that become? It is a serious offense.

If a monk makes preparations against someone who has become fully ordained and kills that fully ordained person, what does that become? It is a defeat.

If a monk makes preparations against someone who has become fully ordained and kills one who is not fully ordained, what does that become? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable one, can it be that there are cases in which a monk who has entered alone into a room meets with conditions that separate him from life and yet incurs a defeat? There are. In the following cases: a monk makes preparations to kill and then enters a room, or, having entered a room, proceeds to make preparations to kill.

Venerable one, can it be that there are cases in which a monk, perceiving a human being as not human, kills with the intention to kill, yet even though that person is separated from life, no defeat is incurred? There are. In the case of one in whom a prior downfall has arisen, one who resides by stealth, one who was inimical from the beginning, a paṇḍaka, or a nun who has been expelled — in those cases it becomes a wrong deed.

Just as the conditions that separate a monk from life with a defeat — the same applies to one who has given back the training precepts, and to a nun: it becomes a defeat. For a novice nun, a novice monk, and a female novice, it is a wrong deed.

Summary verse:

A buddha, the Teacher, one who sees clearly,
a non-learner, a learner, and anything at all —
one who has lapsed, one who is liberated, one who is frightened,
one who is born and one who is attained are similar.

The venerable Ñepa Khor addressed the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has spoken of a monk who, without directly knowing or fully knowing, does not have and does not possess the summit of distinctly human qualities — the noble and distinguished state of genuine knowledge, vision, and abiding in direct experience — and yet makes a claim such as "I know this, I see this," and from such a false claim a downfall has arisen. Wishing to purify that downfall, he says at another time, whether questioned or not questioned, these words to the venerable ones: "I spoke without knowing; I said 'I see' when I had not seen — I spoke falsehood and lies." If it is then said that apart from the conceit of superiority, that monk too incurs a defeat and should not be allowed to remain — what exactly does this mean?

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I am a buddha" — what does that become? If, having pondered it from the standpoint of having internalized evil and unwholesome qualities, or from the standpoint of conventional designation, he makes such a declaration, it becomes a serious offense. But if he declares it in terms of qualities that are absent in a buddha, it becomes a defeat.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I am a Teacher" — what does that become? If he speaks having thought, "I am showing instructions, guiding followers, teaching the Dharma," it becomes a serious offense. But if he makes the declaration in terms of the qualities of the Teacher, it becomes a defeat.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I am a śrāvaka of the Blessed One Vipaśyin, of the one with the crown protrusion, of the destroyer of cyclic existence, of the protector of all, of Kanakamuni, or of Kāśyapa" — what does that become? If he makes a declaration on the basis of a former existence, it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks, "From this time forward I go for refuge to the perfectly and completely awakened buddha Śākyamuni" —

and from that point forward he thinks, "I go for refuge to all the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas" — then if he says this, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I am one beyond training" — what does that become? If he declares this as a prophecy that he has abandoned the afflictions, it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks, "I am not going to train in any craft whatsoever," and says this, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I am one in training" — what does that become? If he declares this as a prophecy that he is engaged in training, it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks, "I am training in all manner of conduct," and says this, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I have nothing whatsoever" — what does that become? If he declares, "I have abandoned everything whatsoever," it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks, "I have no alms-bowl, no Dharma robe, no requisite for the ascetic's livelihood — nothing at all," and says this, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I myself am fallen" — what does that become? If he says, "I am fallen from the fruits of the ascetic's conduct," it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks of a falling from scripture and says this, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I am liberated" — what does that become? If he says, "I am liberated from engagements, bonds, latent tendencies, secondary afflictions, and entanglements," it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks, "Those engagements, bonds, latent tendencies, secondary afflictions, and entanglements that are exhausted, ceased, separated, and transformed — I am liberated from those," and says this, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I am without fear" — what does that become? If he declares this as a prophecy that he has abandoned fear, it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks of worldly matters and says, "I fear nothing whatsoever," it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "This is my final birth" — what does that become? If he declares this as a prophecy of exhaustion, it becomes a defeat. But if he thinks, "This birth — the one of which this is the final birth — is exhausted, ceased, separated, and transformed," and says this, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "I have attained" — what does that become? If he says, "I have attained the fruits of the ascetic's conduct," it becomes a defeat. But if he declares it in terms of something worldly, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says these words: "Alas, you — however you are, just as you are — what is the matter? If you were a stream-enterer (Skt. srotāpanna), a once-returner (Skt. sakṛdāgāmin), a non-returner (Skt. anāgāmin), or an arhat, then I am neither a stream-enterer, nor a once-returner, nor a non-returner, nor an arhat" — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

The summary verse:

Unripened attainment, questioning oneself,
abandonment, and the two fruits,
the four perceptions, what is to be attained,
the worldly way, and the final — these are the topics.

Venerable one, if a monk is asked by an elder, "Are you one whose defilements are exhausted?" —

and he too held unripened fruits of the tree, and when asked said, "It is so" — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says, "I have attained the fruit of stream-entry, the fruit of once-returning, the fruit of non-returning, and the supreme fruit" — what does that become? It becomes a final offense.

Venerable one, if a monk questions himself and then says, "I have asked whether I am an arhat" — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a householder asks a monk, "Have you attained a fruit?" and the monk holds leaves, flowers, and unripened fruits, and then says, "I have" — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says, "I myself have entirely fallen away" — what does that become? If he declares that he has fallen away from something he has actually attained, it becomes a final offense. But if he declares that he has fallen away from something he has not attained, it becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk, thinking he has been prophesied as a stream-enterer, declares the fruit of once-returning; thinking he has been prophesied as a once-returner, declares the fruit of non-returning; thinking he has been prophesied as a non-returner, declares the fruit of arhatship — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says, "At Wandering Mountain there is the fruit of stream-entry; on the face of the Bird-Shelter there is the fruit of once-returning; on Vulture's Peak there is the fruit of non-returning; and in the Pale Forest there is the supreme fruit, arhatship" — what does that become? If he thinks, "There my instructions were given, I recited them, and I did not make an assertion," and speaks on that basis, it becomes a serious offense. But if he declares the fruits of the ascetic life in reference to those places, it becomes a final offense.

Venerable one, if a monk perceives a non-human as a non-human and declares that a superhuman quality has arisen in him — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense. If he perceives a non-human as a human and declares that a superhuman quality has arisen — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense. If he perceives a human as a non-human and declares that a superhuman quality has arisen — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense. If he perceives a human as a human and declares that a superhuman quality has arisen — what does that become? It becomes a final offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says, "Whatever is to be attained as a śrāvaka ground, that I have attained" — what does that become? If he speaks thinking of something to be attained through awakening, it becomes a serious offense. But if he declares the fruits in terms of śrāvaka qualities, it becomes a final offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says, "Today I did not enter into a worldly meditative absorption, nor did I enter it at dusk, nor have I entered it up to this point" — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk says, "I have declared the fruits right here" — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

The Supreme Text of the Vinaya, second fascicle.

The summary verse:

Through a messenger, through a letter, through gesture,
through ascribing an expression to another as intermediary,
through madness, through a disturbed mind,
through distress from sensation, through stupidity.

Venerable one, if a monk communicates a superhuman quality through a messenger — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Likewise, through a letter, and through ascribing an expression to another as intermediary, if one declares a superhuman quality — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk declares a superhuman quality in the presence of a mad person — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Likewise, if one declares a superhuman quality in the presence of one with a disturbed mind, one in distress from sensation, one who is deaf, or one who is stupid — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

The summary verse:

Asleep, delirious, utterly intoxicated,
in meditative absorption, in a non-human destiny,
through speech, through body, in a secluded place, through mantra,
from another and for another's sake — these are the final ones.

Venerable one, if a monk declares a superhuman quality in the presence of one who is asleep — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Likewise, if one declares a superhuman quality in the presence of one who is delirious, one who is utterly intoxicated, one who has entered meditative absorption, or one dwelling in another destiny — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk from the central region speaks a superhuman quality in the presence of a person from the central region, using the dialect of a border region, and the person from the central region does not understand that border dialect — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

If a person from a border region speaks a superhuman quality in the presence of a person from a border region, using the dialect of the central region, and the person from the border region does not understand the dialect of the central region — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

If a person from a border region speaks a superhuman quality in the presence of a person from the central region, using a border dialect, and the person from the central region does not understand that border dialect — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

If a person from the central region speaks a superhuman quality in the presence of a person from a border region, using the dialect of the central region, and the person from the border region does not understand the dialect of the central region — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk declares a superhuman quality through bodily gesture — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a secluded place as secluded, declares a superhuman quality — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

If, perceiving a secluded place as not secluded, one declares a superhuman quality — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

If, perceiving a non-secluded place as secluded, one declares a superhuman quality — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

If, perceiving a non-secluded place as not secluded, one declares a superhuman quality — what does that become? It becomes a final offense.

Venerable one, if a monk, having made himself unrecognizable through secret mantra or medicines, declares a superhuman quality — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk declares as his own a superhuman quality that belongs to another — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, if a monk declares a superhuman quality on behalf of others — what does that become? It becomes a serious offense.

Venerable one, are there cases where a monk declares a superhuman quality and yet it does not become a final offense? There are. One who has previously committed a downfall, one who dwells by way of theft, one who was originally of contrary nature, a hermaphrodite, and one who has been expelled from the community of nuns — for these it becomes a wrongdoing.

Just as through the condition of a monk speaking falsely it becomes a final offense, in the same way it becomes a final offense for one who has surrendered the training and for a fully ordained nun. For a female probationer, a male novice, and a female novice, it is a wrongdoing.

Requested by Upāli. The attachment of the root defeat from the Kāśyapīya school is complete.

The summary verse:

Without delusion, in concealment, and likewise through movement,
those without effort, and the marks,
those belonging to another included here —

correctly stated in the assembly.

Upāli, the attendant, asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has taught that if one intentionally causes seminal emission — except in a dream — it is a remainder for the Saṅgha. What is meant by that?"

"Venerable one, if a monk begins while not yet asleep and then falls asleep and a seminal emission occurs, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk begins while asleep and then awakens and a seminal emission occurs, what does that become?"

"If effort is made, it becomes a grave offense. But if no effort is made, there is no transgression."

"Venerable one, regarding what the Blessed One has taught — that if one causes a seminal emission that has entered into the sheath it becomes a grave offense — how far must it have entered the sheath?"

"From its resting position it becomes a case of having entered the sheath as soon as it has advanced."

"Venerable one, regarding what the Blessed One has taught — that if one causes a seminal emission by setting it in motion it becomes a grave offense — how much movement constitutes setting it in motion?"

"Remaining in contact with what follows, it is a case of having been set in motion once it has advanced from its resting position."

"Venerable one, if a seminal emission occurs without any striving, what does that become?"

"If one does not appropriate it, there is no transgression. But if one does appropriate it, it becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk undergoes a change of marks through causing a seminal emission and becomes a nun, what does that become?"

"It becomes a transgression."

"Venerable one, if a nun undergoes a change of marks through causing a seminal emission and becomes a monk, what does that become?"

"It becomes a remainder for the Saṅgha."

"Venerable one, if a monk causes another to become an outflow and thereby brings about a seminal emission, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, are there cases where a monk who intentionally causes a seminal emission does not incur a remainder for the Saṅgha?"

"Yes — one in whom a prior transgression has already arisen, one who is in a state of theft, one who from the outset is of incompatible nature, a paṇḍaka, and a nun who has been expelled: for these it becomes a wrongdoing."

"Just as a monk who causes a seminal emission incurs a remainder for the Saṅgha, so likewise does one who has surrendered the training. For a fully ordained nun it is according to the same basis. For a female probationer, a male novice, and a female novice, it is a wrongdoing."

The summary verse:

Body and mind — those are two — and likewise
wounds, hairs, and nails alike;
the fourth, if done in two ways:
these are correctly stated in the assembly.

Upāli, the attendant, asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has further taught that if a monk, with a mind of passion and agitation, makes bodily contact with a woman — grasping her hand, grasping her upper arm, grasping her braid, or touching and rubbing any limb, major or minor — and does so himself, it is a remainder for the Saṅgha. What is meant by that?"

"Venerable one, if a woman's bodily faculties have deteriorated and a monk makes bodily contact with her, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if one whose bodily faculties have deteriorated makes bodily contact with a woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if the bodily faculties of both have deteriorated and they make bodily contact with each other, what does that become?"

"It becomes a wrongdoing."

"Venerable one, if a monk, while entertaining doubt and uncertainty about whether this is such a person or not, nonetheless makes bodily contact with that person, what does that become?"

"It becomes a remainder for the Saṅgha."

"Venerable one, if a monk, while entertaining doubt and uncertainty about whether this is a woman or not a woman, nonetheless makes bodily contact with that person, what does that become?"

"It is according to the actual facts of the matter."

"Venerable one, if contact is made through a garment, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if contact is made with teeth, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if contact is made with a wound, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if contact is made with wounds, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if contact is made with a single hair, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if contact is made with hairs, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if contact is made with a single nail, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if contact is made with nails, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a human woman as human, makes bodily contact with her, what does that become?"

"It becomes a remainder for the Saṅgha."

"If he perceives one who is human as not human, and makes bodily contact, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he perceives one who is not human as human, and makes bodily contact, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he perceives one who is not human as not human, and makes bodily contact, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving one with an intervening object as having an intervening object, makes bodily contact, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he perceives one with an intervening object as having no intervening object, and makes bodily contact, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he perceives one with no intervening object as having an intervening object, and makes bodily contact, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he perceives one with no intervening object as having no intervening object, and makes bodily contact, what does that become?"

"It becomes a remainder for the Saṅgha."

Summary verse:

Perception, another's corpse and bones,
marks, male, soft, warm,
a woman, a hermaphrodite, a madwoman,
meditative absorption, sleep, not a being.

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact with a woman who has developed attachment for another man, together with that other man, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact together with the corpse of a woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a remainder for the Saṅgha."

"Venerable one, if bodily contact is made together with a skeleton from which the flesh has gone, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact with a woman and, as a result of that contact, she attains the marks of a monk — if she becomes a monk — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a nun makes bodily contact with a man and, as a result of that contact, she attains the marks of a man — if she becomes a monk — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact together with a man, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact with the thought that this might be a relative, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"With a gentle intention or a warm intention — it is the same in those cases as well."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact together with a hermaphrodite, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If bodily contact is made together with a female hermaphrodite, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact together with a mad woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Likewise, if bodily contact is made together with one whose mind is deranged, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact together with a woman who is in meditative absorption, what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact together with a woman who is asleep, what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk makes bodily contact together with a woman who belongs to another form of existence, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, are there cases where a monk makes bodily contact and yet it does not become a Saṅgha-remainder offense?"

"There are. These are: one in whom a prior transgression has already arisen; one who abides in the guise of a thief; one who is initially incompatible; a hermaphrodite; and a nun who has been expelled — in those cases it is merely a wrongdoing."

Just as a monk who makes bodily contact throughout incurs a Saṅgha-remainder offense, in the same way, one who has surrendered the training also incurs a Saṅgha-remainder offense. A monk who makes full bodily contact together with a woman who has not yet been born incurs a defeat. In the case of a female trainee, a novice monk, and a novice nun, it becomes a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

By voice, by body, in seclusion, by mantra,
emanating from one place to appear in another,
by messenger, by letter, by hand-signal,
conveying a proposition through an intermediary,
by arrangement — a hermaphrodite male, and
a female hermaphrodite who is charming: these are offenses.

The venerable Upāli (the attendant of the assembly) asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One declared that a monk who, with a mind aroused and agitated, toward a woman as the object, utters vile words of indecency that are assembled — conjoined with lewdness, even speaking as a man would speak to a young woman — incurs a Saṅgha-remainder offense. What exactly is that?"

"Venerable one, if a monk from the central region addresses a woman from the central region in the dialect of a border region with words of indecency, and that woman from the central region does not understand the border dialect, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If a monk from a border region addresses a woman from a border region in the dialect of the central region with words of indecency, and that woman from the border region does not understand the dialect of the central region, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If a monk from a border region addresses a woman from the central region in a border dialect with words of indecency, and that woman from the central region does not understand the border dialect, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If a monk from the central region addresses a woman from a border region in the dialect of the central region with words of indecency, and that woman from the border region does not understand the dialect of the central region, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk conveys words of indecency to a woman by means of bodily gesture, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving seclusion to be seclusion, speaks words of indecency to a woman in that secluded place, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If, perceiving what is seclusion to be not secluded, he speaks words of indecency, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If, perceiving what is not secluded to be secluded, he speaks words of indecency, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If, perceiving what is not secluded to be not secluded, he speaks words of indecency to a woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk disguises himself with medicine or a secret mantra so as to be unrecognizable, and then speaks words of indecency to a woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency to a woman while at a distance from her, through another person, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency to a woman on behalf of others, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency to an emanated woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency about a woman by way of a messenger, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"And likewise if it is done through a written letter, a hand signal, making an insinuation to another, or speaking indirectly — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk says to a woman, 'Make love with me,' what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks such words to a paṇḍaka, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he speaks them to a man, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he speaks them to a female paṇḍaka, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk says to a woman, 'You are beautiful,' what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk says to a woman, 'You are wicked,' what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

Summary verse:
One who is mad, or mentally disturbed,
or overcome by feeling, or a deaf-mute, or a fool,
or asleep, or at work, or thoroughly intoxicated,
or a nun who has entered into absorption —

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency to a woman who is mad, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"And likewise if she is mentally disturbed, or overcome by feeling, or a deaf-mute, or a fool, or asleep, or occupied with work, or thoroughly intoxicated — if he speaks words of indecency to her, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, a monk who is in the act of speaking words of indecency to a woman — if his own marks change and he becomes a nun, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, a nun who is in the act of speaking words of indecency to a man — if her marks change and she becomes a monk, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, the Blessed One also declared: 'Whatever monk speaks words of indecency directed at a woman — that becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense.' Is there any case in which a monk might speak words of indecency directed at a woman and it would not become a Saṅgha-remainder offense?"

"There is. If he speaks them on another's behalf, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency to a woman who has entered into meditative absorption, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

Summary verse:

and what has been declared regarding indecency —
the same applies to solicitation for service.
This is to be explained in full detail.

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency to a human woman while perceiving her as not human, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he speaks words of indecency to a non-human female while perceiving her as human, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he speaks words of indecency to a human woman while perceiving her as human, what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk speaks words of indecency to a woman while perceiving her as a woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"If he speaks words of indecency to a woman while perceiving her as a man, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he speaks words of indecency to a man while perceiving him as a woman, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he speaks words of indecency to a man while perceiving him as a man, what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, are there cases in which a monk speaks words of indecency directed at a woman and yet it does not become a Saṅgha-remainder offense?"

"There are. In the following cases it becomes a wrongdoing: when a transgression has already arisen before, when one dwells in a state of concealment, when there is an initial incompatibility, when it concerns a pandaka, and when it concerns an expelled nun."

"Just as speaking words of indecency to a monk becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense, so too does it become a Saṅgha-remainder offense when directed at one who has received the training. In the case of a fully ordained nun, it becomes a grave offense. In the case of a female trainee-nun, a novice monk, or a novice nun, it becomes a wrongdoing."

The venerable Upāli then addressed the Blessed One, the buddha Bhagavān: "Venerable Blessed One, you also said it is a Saṅgha-remainder offense when a monk, with a mind that is passionate and inflamed, speaks praise of service to himself in the presence of those attending upon a woman, saying something of this kind: 'A monk such as I, possessed of moral discipline and wholesome qualities, living the pure life — for one to render service to me by engaging in this act of union with me, that is the highest.' What was meant by the statement that it becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense?"

"The remainder of the text is to be expounded just as it was set forth in the case of speaking words of indecency, including the praise of service. The one difference is this: in the case of a fully ordained nun speaking praise of service, it is to be declared a wrongdoing. In the case of a female trainee-nun, a novice monk, or a novice nun, it is a wrongdoing."

Summary verse:

Two Dharmas, and then a group of six,
soliciting and purchasing — those two,
transferring, and then two groups of four:
eight in all, and these are the last.

The venerable Upāli then addressed the Blessed One, the buddha Bhagavān: "Venerable one, what is the Dharma that is neither past, nor future, nor present, has no going or coming, and has reached the ultimate end of the reach of karma?"

"Venerable Upāli, it is nirvāṇa."

"Venerable one, what is the Dharma that does have going and coming, and has reached the ultimate end of the reach of karma?"

"Venerable Upāli, it is one who speaks in jest."

"And if one does that, what does it become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"The Blessed One also said it is a Saṅgha-remainder offense when a monk praises himself to a woman using masculine terms, or praises himself to a man using feminine terms, representing himself as a husband or a lover — and that even a single moment of this suffices. What was meant by the statement that it becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense?"

"Venerable one, if a monk accepts a commission from a madwoman, delivers it to the madwoman, and then conveys the reply back — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from a madwoman, delivers it to the madwoman, and then conveys the reply back to one in her natural state — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from a madwoman, delivers it to one in her natural state, and then conveys the reply back to the madwoman — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from a madwoman, delivers it to one in her natural state, and then conveys the reply back to one in her natural state — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk accepts a commission from a woman in her natural state, delivers it to a woman in her natural state, and then conveys the reply back to one in her natural state — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"If he accepts a commission from a woman in her natural state, delivers it to a woman in her natural state, and then conveys the reply back to a madwoman — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk accepts a commission from one in her natural state, delivers it to a madwoman, and then conveys the reply back to one in her natural state — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from one in her natural state, delivers it to a madwoman, and then conveys the reply back to a madwoman — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

The two sets of four cases involving a madwoman apply in the same way to a mentally disturbed woman, and likewise the two sets of four cases apply to a woman overcome by feeling.

"Venerable one, regarding a householder who is capable and one who is not capable — first, if a monk accepts a commission from one who is capable, delivers it to one who is capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"If he accepts a commission from one who is capable, delivers it to one who is capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is not capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from one who is capable, delivers it to one who is not capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from one who is capable, delivers it to one who is not capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is not capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk accepts a commission from one who is not capable, delivers it to one who is not capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is not capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a misdeed."

"If he accepts a commission from one who is not capable, delivers it to one who is not capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from one who is not capable, delivers it to one who is capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is not capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If he accepts a commission from one who is not capable, delivers it to one who is capable, and then conveys the reply back to one who is capable — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk says to a householder, 'Buy a woman,' what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk says to a householder, 'Buy the woman called Such-and-such,' what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable one, if one who has not yet received full ordination acts as a go-between for a marriage proposal, delivers it to one who has not yet received full ordination, and then conveys the reply back to one who has not yet received full ordination — what does that become?"

"It becomes a misdeed."

"If one who has not yet received full ordination acts as a go-between for a marriage proposal, delivers it to one who has not yet received full ordination, and then conveys the reply back to one in the process of receiving full ordination — what does that become?"

"It becomes a misdeed."

"If one who has not yet received full ordination acts as a go-between for a marriage proposal, delivers it to one who has not yet received full ordination, and then conveys the reply back to one who has fully received ordination — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If one in the process of receiving full ordination acts as a go-between for a marriage proposal, delivers it to one in the process of receiving full ordination, and then conveys the reply back to one who has since received full ordination — what does that become?"

"It becomes a misdeed."

"If one in the process of receiving full ordination acts as a go-between for a marriage proposal, delivers it to one in the process of receiving full ordination, and then conveys the reply back to one who has fully received ordination — what does that become?"

"It becomes a grave offense."

"If one who has fully received ordination acts as a go-between for a marriage proposal, delivers it to one who has fully received ordination, and then conveys the reply back to one who has fully received ordination — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"If one who has fully received ordination acts as a go-between for a marriage proposal, delivers it to one who has fully received ordination, and then conveys the reply back to one who has not yet received full ordination — what does that become?"

"It becomes a misdeed."

"Venerable one, this may refer to the same person being the one who is moved, and also to having been moved by the same person."

It also seems that there is the practice of acting to satisfy that very same person, and also the practice of having satisfied that very same person. Yes, it is so. Consider this case: a person who has engaged in the practice of acting to please another, to whom the Saṅgha has not yet assigned a Saṅgha-remainder offense, goes before the Saṅgha and requests that the act of satisfaction be performed. The Saṅgha grants that person the act of satisfaction. Now, while that person was largely performing the act of satisfaction, two Saṅgha-remainder offenses arose: the first was assigned after one day, and the second was assigned after two days. That person then goes before the Saṅgha and requests a transfer. If the Saṅgha grants the transfer, then the first offense is one that has been committed, the second is one for which a transfer has been given, the third is one involving the practice of acting to satisfy, and the fourth is one involving the practice of having satisfied.

"Venerable one, suppose neither a woman nor a man is involved, and a monk acts as go-between for the two of them — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"The female is not a human being, and the male is not a human being, and a monk acts as go-between for the two of them — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"The female is not a human being, and the male is a human being, and a monk acts as go-between for the two of them — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"The female is also not a human being, and the male is also not a human being, and a monk acts as go-between for the two of them — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk acts as go-between while perceiving human beings as human beings — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"If a monk acts as go-between while perceiving human beings as non-human beings — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If a monk acts as go-between while perceiving non-human beings as human beings — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If a monk acts as go-between while perceiving non-human beings as non-human beings — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk acts as go-between indirectly — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk acts as go-between for householders who have no sons — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk acts as go-between for a wife — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If a monk acts as go-between for persons of indeterminate sex — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"Venerable one, a certain householder goes before the assembled Saṅgha and says: 'Noble ones, please use my words to say the following to a certain senior householder: Please give my son a daughter, a sister, a niece, or a grand-niece.' If the entire Saṅgha accepts this message and delivers it and conveys the reply, what does it become for the entire Saṅgha?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense for the entire Saṅgha."

"But if, at the behest of the entire Saṅgha, a single monk delivers it and conveys the reply — what does it become for the entire Saṅgha?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense for the entire Saṅgha."

"But if a single monk delivers it and conveys the reply on his own authority — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If a monk acts as go-between for nuns and for others observing celibacy who abide in the training — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"Venerable one, when a monk, having just accepted a message,

delivers it and conveys the reply to a nun — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a nun has just accepted a message, and in that moment her sex changes, so that she becomes a monk, and he then delivers it and conveys the reply — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a monk is acting as go-between and the woman in question acquires male characteristics, or a man acquires female characteristics, and while still in the process of changing sex he delivers the message and conveys the reply — what does that become?"

"It becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense."

"Venerable one, if a man has arranged a signal with a woman, and that man says to a monk, 'Venerable one, would you look in on the woman?' — and the monk says, 'She is sitting right there at the foot of that tree' — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"Venerable one, are there cases in which a monk acts as go-between yet it does not become a Saṅgha-remainder offense?"

"There are. If a prior downfall has occurred; if one is dwelling in concealment; if one was initially incompatible; if one is a paṇḍaka; if the nun has been expelled — in these cases it becomes a wrongdoing. Just as with the monk acting as go-between it becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense, so too for one who has returned the training and for a nun it becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense. For a probationer, a novice monk, and a novice nun it becomes a wrongdoing."

Summary verse:
Three bases, the standard measure,
oneself and another, and the uncovered,
